Tupper">Charles Tupper</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Succeeded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a Party</a></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b>In office</b></span><br />June 2, 1887&#160;–&#32;February 17, 1919</td><link </td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Henri Charles Wilfrid Laurier</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1841-11-20</span>)</span>November 20, 1841<br /><a href="/wiki/Saint-Lin%E2%80%93Laurentides" title="Saint-Lin–Laurentides">Saint-Lin</a>, Canada East</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">February 17, 1919<span style="display:none">(1919-02-17)</span> (aged&#160;77)<br /><a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a>, Canada</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/Notre-Dame_Cemetery_(Ottawa)" title="Notre-Dame Cemetery (Ottawa)">Notre Dame Cemetery</a>, Ottawa, Ontario</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Other political<br />affiliations</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Laurier_Liberals" title="Laurier Liberals">Laurier Liberal</a> (1917–1919)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Laurier" title="Zoé Laurier">Zoé Lafontaine</a></div>&#32;<div 1864)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Profession</th><td class="infobox-data">Lawyer</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilfrid_Laurier_Signature2.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Wilfrid Laurier&#39;s signature"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Wilfrid_Laurier_Signature2.svg/128px-Wilfrid_Laurier_Signature2.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="28" class="mw-file-element" militia</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Years&#160;of service</th><td class="infobox-data">1869–1878</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Rank</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Ensign_(rank)" title="Ensign (rank)">Ensign</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Unit</th><td class="infobox-data">Arthabaskaville Infantry Company</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Battles/wars</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Fenian_Raids" class="mw-redirect" title="Fenian Raids">Fenian Raids</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Sir Henri Charles Wilfrid Laurier</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">GCMG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/King%27s_Counsel" title="King&#39;s Counsel">KC</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/ɒr/: &#39;or&#39; in &#39;moral&#39;">ɒr</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">LORR</span>-ee-ay</i></a>; <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small">French:</span> <span class="IPA nowrap" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">&#91;wilfʁid<span class="wrap"> </span>loʁje&#93;</a></span>; November 20, 1841&#160;– February 17, 1919) was a Canadian lawyer, statesman, and politician who served as the seventh <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1896 to 1911. The first <a href="/wiki/French_Canadians" title="French Canadians">French Canadian</a> prime minister, his 15-year tenure remains the longest unbroken term of office among Canadian prime ministers and his nearly 45 years of service in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a> is a record for the House. Laurier is best known for his compromises between <a href="/wiki/English_Canada" title="English Canada">English</a> and French Canada.
</p><p>Laurier studied law at <a href="/wiki/McGill_University" title="McGill University">McGill University</a> and practised as a lawyer before being elected to the <a href="/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Legislative Assembly of Quebec">Legislative Assembly of Quebec</a> in <a href="/wiki/1871_Quebec_general_election" title="1871 Quebec general election">1871</a>. He was then elected as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">member of Parliament</a> (MP) in the <a href="/wiki/1874_Canadian_federal_election" title="1874 Canadian federal election">1874 federal election</a>. As an MP, Laurier gained a large personal following among French Canadians and the <a href="/wiki/Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_people" title="Québécois people">Québécois</a>. He also came to be known as a great orator. After serving as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_National_Revenue#Minister_of_Inland_Revenue" title="Minister of National Revenue">minister of inland revenue</a> under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Mackenzie_(politician)" title="Alexander Mackenzie (politician)">Alexander Mackenzie</a> from 1877 to 1878, Laurier became leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party</a> in 1887, thus becoming <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Official Opposition</a>. He lost the <a href="/wiki/1891_Canadian_federal_election" title="1891 Canadian federal election">1891 federal election</a> to Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(1867%E2%80%931942)" title="Conservative Party of Canada (1867–1942)">Conservatives</a>. However, controversy surrounding the Conservative government's handling of the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question" title="Manitoba Schools Question">Manitoba Schools Question</a>, which was triggered by the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba" title="Manitoba">Manitoba</a> government's elimination of funding for <a href="/wiki/Catholic_school" title="Catholic school">Catholic schools</a>, gave Laurier a victory in the <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896 federal election</a>. He paved the Liberal Party to three more election victories afterwards.
</p><p>As prime minister, Laurier solved the Manitoba Schools Question by allowing Catholic students to have a Catholic education on a school-by-school basis. Despite his controversial handling of the dispute and criticism from some French Canadians who believed that the resolution was insufficient, he was nicknamed "the Great Conciliator" for offering a compromise between French and English Canada. Two issues, the United Kingdom demanding Canadian military support to fight in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a>, and the United Kingdom asking Canada to send money for the <a href="/wiki/British_Navy" class="mw-redirect" title="British Navy">British Navy</a>, divided the country as English Canadians supported Britain's requests whereas French Canadians did not. Laurier's government sought a middle ground between the two groups, deciding to send a <a href="/wiki/Volunteer_military" title="Volunteer military">volunteer force</a> to fight in the Boer War and passing the 1910 <i><a href="/wiki/Naval_Service_Act" title="Naval Service Act">Naval Service Act</a></i> to create Canada's own <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Navy" title="Royal Canadian Navy">navy</a>. In addition, his government dramatically increased <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada" title="Immigration to Canada">immigration</a>, oversaw <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saskatchewan" title="Saskatchewan">Saskatchewan</a>'s entry into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>, constructed the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Pacific" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Trunk Pacific">Grand Trunk Pacific</a> and <a href="/wiki/National_Transcontinental_Railway" title="National Transcontinental Railway">National Transcontinental</a> Railways, and put effort into establishing Canada as an autonomous country within the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>.
</p><p>Laurier's proposed <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(Canadian_politics)" title="Reciprocity (Canadian politics)">reciprocity</a> agreement with the United States to lower tariffs became a main issue in the <a href="/wiki/1911_Canadian_federal_election" title="1911 Canadian federal election">1911 federal election</a>, in which the Liberals were defeated by the Conservatives led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Borden" title="Robert Borden">Robert Borden</a>, who claimed that the treaty would lead to the US influencing Canadian identity. Despite his defeat, Laurier stayed on as Liberal leader and once again became leader of the Opposition. During <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a>, Laurier faced divisions within the Liberal Party as pro-<a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a> Liberals <a href="/wiki/Liberal%E2%80%93Unionist" title="Liberal–Unionist">joined</a> Borden's <a href="/wiki/Unionist_Party_(Canada)" title="Unionist Party (Canada)">Unionist government</a>. The anti-conscription faction of the Liberal Party, led by Laurier, became the <a href="/wiki/Laurier_Liberals" title="Laurier Liberals">Laurier Liberals</a>, though the group would be heavily defeated by Borden's Unionists in the <a href="/wiki/1917_Canadian_federal_election" title="1917 Canadian federal election">1917 federal election</a>. Laurier remained Opposition leader even after his 1917 defeat, but was not able to fight in another election as he died in 1919. Laurier is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">ranked</a> among the top three of Canadian prime ministers. At 31 years and 8 months, Laurier is the longest-serving leader of a major Canadian political party. He is the fourth-longest serving prime minister of Canada, behind <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>, Macdonald, and <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life_.281841.E2.80.931871.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_(1841–1871)">Early life (1841–1871)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life (1841–1871)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Laurier_bedroom,_Saint-Lin-Laurentides,_Quebec.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg/220px-Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg/330px-Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg/440px-Laurier_bedroom%2C_Saint-Lin-Laurentides%2C_Quebec.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3840" data-file-height="2160" /></a><figcaption>Bedroom at Sir Wilfrid Laurier National Historic Site, <a href="/wiki/Saint-Lin-Laurentides" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint-Lin-Laurentides">Saint-Lin-Laurentides</a>, Quebec</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Childhood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The second child of Carolus Laurier and Marcelle Martineau, Henri Charles Wilfrid Laurier was born in Saint-Lin, <a href="/wiki/Canada_East" title="Canada East">Canada East</a> (modern-day <a href="/wiki/Saint-Lin-Laurentides" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint-Lin-Laurentides">Saint-Lin-Laurentides</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>), on November 20, 1841. He was a sixth-generation <a href="/wiki/French_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="French Canadian">French Canadian</a>. His ancestor François Cottineau, <i>dit</i> Champlaurier, came to Canada from <a href="/wiki/Saint-Claud" title="Saint-Claud">Saint-Claud</a>, France. Laurier grew up in a family where politics was a staple of talk and debate. His father, an educated man having liberal ideas, enjoyed a certain degree of prestige about town. In addition to being a farmer and <a href="/wiki/Surveying" title="Surveying">surveyor</a>, he also occupied such sought-after positions as mayor, <a href="/wiki/Justice_of_the_peace" title="Justice of the peace">justice of the peace</a>, militia lieutenant and <a href="/wiki/School_board" class="mw-redirect" title="School board">school board</a> member. At the age of 11, Wilfrid left home to study in <a href="/wiki/New_Glasgow,_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="New Glasgow, Quebec">New Glasgow, Quebec</a>, a neighbouring village largely inhabited by immigrants from <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>. Over the next two years, he familiarized himself with the mentality, language and culture of British people, in addition to learning English. In 1854, Laurier attended the Collège de L'Assomption, an institution that staunchly followed <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholicism">Roman Catholicism</a>. There, he started to develop an interest in politics, and began to endorse the ideology of <a href="/wiki/Liberalism_in_Canada" title="Liberalism in Canada">liberalism</a>, despite the school being heavily <a href="/wiki/Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Conservatism in Canada">conservative</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_beginnings">Political beginnings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Political beginnings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In September 1861, Laurier began studying law at <a href="/wiki/McGill_University" title="McGill University">McGill University</a>. There, he met <a href="/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Lafontaine" class="mw-redirect" title="Zoé Lafontaine">Zoé Lafontaine</a>, who would later become his wife. Laurier also discovered that he had chronic <a href="/wiki/Bronchitis" title="Bronchitis">bronchitis</a>, an illness that would stick with him for the rest of his life. At McGill, Laurier joined the <a href="/wiki/Parti_rouge" title="Parti rouge">Parti Rouge</a>, or Red Party, which was a <a href="/wiki/Centre-left_politics" title="Centre-left politics">centre-left</a> political party that contested elections in Canada East. In 1864, Laurier graduated from McGill. Laurier would continue being active within the Parti Rouge, and from May 1864 to fall 1866, served as vice president of the <a href="/wiki/Institut_canadien_de_Montr%C3%A9al" title="Institut canadien de Montréal">Institut canadien de Montréal</a>, a literary society with ties to the Rouge. In August 1864, Laurier joined the Liberals of Lower Canada, an anti-<a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a> group composed of both moderates and radicals. The group argued that Confederation would give too much power to the central, or federal government, and the group believed that Confederation would lead to discrimination towards French Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_in_his_student_days,_circa_1865_in_the_formatof_a_carte-de-visite_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/00/Portrait_of_Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_in_his_student_days%2C_circa_1865_in_the_formatof_a_carte-de-visite_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="116" height="142" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="116" data-file-height="142" /></a><figcaption>Laurier in his student days</figcaption></figure>
<p>Laurier then practised law in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, though he initially struggled as a lawyer. He opened his first practice on October 27, 1864, but closed it within a month. He established his second office, but that closed within three months, due to a lack of clients. In March 1865, nearly bankrupt, Laurier established his third law firm, partnering with Médéric Lanctot, a lawyer and journalist who staunchly opposed Confederation. The two experienced some success, but in late 1866, Laurier was invited by fellow Rouge <a href="/wiki/Antoine-Aim%C3%A9_Dorion" title="Antoine-Aimé Dorion">Antoine-Aimé Dorion</a> to replace his recently deceased brother to became editor and run the newspaper, <i>Le Défricheur</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier moved to <a href="/wiki/Victoriaville" title="Victoriaville">Victoriaville</a> and began writing and controlling the newspaper from January 1, 1867. Laurier saw this as an opportunity to express his strong anti-Confederation views; in one instance he wrote, "Confederation is the second stage on the road to ‘anglification’ mapped out by <a href="/wiki/John_Lambton,_1st_Earl_of_Durham" title="John Lambton, 1st Earl of Durham">Lord Durham</a>...We are being handed over to the English majority...[We must] use whatever influence we have left to demand and obtain a free and separate government." On March 21, <i>Le Défricheur</i> was forced to shut down, as a result of financial issues and opposition from the local <a href="/wiki/Clergy" title="Clergy">clergy</a>. On July 1, Confederation was officially proclaimed and recognized, a defeat for Laurier.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier decided to remain in Victoriaville. He slowly became well known across the town with a population of 730, and was even elected mayor not so long after he settled. In addition, he established a law practice which would span for three decades and have four different partners. He would make some money, but not enough to consider himself wealthy. During his period in Victoriaville, Laurier opted to accept Confederation and identify himself as a moderate liberal, as opposed to a radical liberal.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> While in Victoriaville, Laurier served as an Ensign in the Arthabaskaville Infantry Company,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> serving from 1869-1878 during the <a href="/wiki/Fenian_Raids" class="mw-redirect" title="Fenian Raids">Fenian Raids</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_political_career_.281871.E2.80.931887.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career_(1871–1887)">Early political career (1871–1887)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early political career (1871–1887)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Member_of_the_Legislative_Assembly_of_Quebec_.281871.E2.80.931874.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_the_Legislative_Assembly_of_Quebec_(1871–1874)">Member of the Legislative Assembly of Quebec (1871–1874)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Member of the Legislative Assembly of Quebec (1871–1874)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A member of the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Liberal_Party" title="Quebec Liberal Party">Quebec Liberal Party</a>, Laurier was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Legislative Assembly of Quebec">Legislative Assembly of Quebec</a> for the riding of <a href="/wiki/Drummond-Arthabaska_(provincial_electoral_district)" title="Drummond-Arthabaska (provincial electoral district)">Drummond-Arthabaska</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1871_Quebec_general_election" title="1871 Quebec general election">1871 Quebec general election</a>, though the Liberal Party altogether suffered a landslide defeat. To win the provincial riding, Laurier campaigned on increasing funding for education, agriculture, and colonization. His career as a provincial politician was not noteworthy, and very few times would he make speeches in the legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Member_of_Parliament_.281874.E2.80.931887.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament_(1874–1887)">Member of Parliament (1874–1887)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament (1874–1887)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Laurier resigned from the provincial legislature to enter federal politics as a <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a>. He was elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1874_Canadian_federal_election" title="1874 Canadian federal election">January 22, 1874 election</a>, representing the riding of <a href="/wiki/Drummond%E2%80%94Arthabaska" title="Drummond—Arthabaska">Drummond—Arthabaska</a>. In this election, the Liberals led by <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Mackenzie_(politician)" title="Alexander Mackenzie (politician)">Alexander Mackenzie</a> heavily triumphed, as a result of the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Scandal" title="Pacific Scandal">Pacific Scandal</a> that was initiated by the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(1867%E2%80%931942)" title="Conservative Party of Canada (1867–1942)">Conservative Party</a> and the Conservative prime minister, <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>. Laurier ran a simple campaign, denouncing Conservative corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilfrid_Laurier,_M.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg/175px-Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg/263px-Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg/350px-Wilfrid_Laurier%2C_M.jpg 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Laurier in 1874</figcaption></figure>
<p>As a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">member of Parliament</a> (MP), Laurier's first mission was to build prominence by giving speeches in the House of Commons. He gained considerable attention when he delivered a speech on political liberalism on June 26, 1877, in front of about 2,000 people. He stated, "Liberal Catholicism is not political liberalism" and that the Liberal Party is not "a party composed of men holding perverse doctrines, with a dangerous tendency, and knowingly and deliberately progressing towards revolution." He also stated, "The policy of the Liberal party is to protect [our] institutions, to defend them and spread them, and, under the sway of those institutions, to develop the country’s latent resources. That is the policy of the Liberal party and it has no other." The speech helped Laurier become a leader of the Quebec wing of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From October 1877 to October 1878, Laurier served briefly in the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Cabinet">Cabinet</a> of Prime Minister Mackenzie as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_National_Revenue#Minister_of_Inland_Revenue" title="Minister of National Revenue">minister of inland revenue</a>. However, his appointment triggered an October 27, 1877 <a href="/wiki/Ministerial_by-election" title="Ministerial by-election">ministerial by-election</a>. In the by-election, he lost his seat in Drummond—Arthabaska. On November 11, he ran for the seat of <a href="/wiki/Quebec_East" title="Quebec East">Quebec East</a>, which he narrowly won. From November 11, 1877, to his death on February 17, 1919, Laurier's seat would be Quebec East. Laurier won reelection for Quebec East in the <a href="/wiki/1878_Canadian_federal_election" title="1878 Canadian federal election">1878 federal election</a>, though the Liberals suffered a landslide defeat as a result of their mishandling of the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1873" title="Panic of 1873">Panic of 1873</a>. Macdonald returned as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier called on Mackenzie to resign as leader, not least because of his handling of the economy. Mackenzie resigned as Liberal leader in 1880 and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Blake" title="Edward Blake">Edward Blake</a>. Laurier, along with others, founded the Quebec newspaper, <i>L’Électeur</i>, to promote the Liberal Party. The Liberals were in opposition once again, and Laurier made use of that status, expressing his support for <a href="/wiki/Laissez-faire" title="Laissez-faire">laissez-faire economics</a> and provincial rights. The Liberals suffered a second consecutive defeat in <a href="/wiki/1882_Canadian_federal_election" title="1882 Canadian federal election">1882</a>, with Macdonald winning his fourth term. Laurier continued to make speeches opposing the Conservative government's policies, though nothing notable came until 1885, when he spoke out against the execution of Métis activist <a href="/wiki/Louis_Riel" title="Louis Riel">Louis Riel</a>, who was hung by Macdonald's government authorities after leading the <a href="/wiki/North-West_Rebellion" title="North-West Rebellion">North-West Rebellion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_.281887.E2.80.931896.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(1887–1896)">Leader of the Official Opposition (1887–1896)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Leader of the Official Opposition (1887–1896)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Edward Blake resigned as Liberal leader after leading them to back-to-back defeats in 1882 and <a href="/wiki/1887_Canadian_federal_election" title="1887 Canadian federal election">1887</a>. Blake urged Laurier to run for leadership of the party. At first, Laurier refused as he was not keen to take such a powerful position, but later on accepted. After 13 and a half years, Laurier had already established his reputation. He was now a prominent politician who was known for leading the Quebec branch of the Liberal Party, known for defending French Canadian rights, and known for being a great orator who was a fierce parliamentary speaker. Over the next nine years, Laurier gradually built up his party's strength through his personal following both in Quebec and elsewhere in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg/150px-Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="210" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg/225px-Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg/300px-Wilfrid_Laurier_1890_-_cropped_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="378" data-file-height="530" /></a><figcaption>Opposition Leader Laurier, 1890</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1891_Canadian_federal_election" title="1891 Canadian federal election">1891 federal election</a>, Laurier faced Conservative Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>. Laurier campaigned in favour of <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(Canadian_politics)" title="Reciprocity (Canadian politics)">reciprocity</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>, with the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>, contrary to Macdonald's position on the matter, who claimed that reciprocity would lead to American annexation of Canada. On election day, March 5, the Liberals gained 10 seats. The Liberals also won a majority of seats in Quebec for the first time since the 1874 election. Prime Minister Macdonald won his fourth consecutive federal election victory. The day after, Blake denounced the Liberal trade policy.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier remained disillusioned for some time after his defeat. Multiple times he suggested he resign as leader, though he was persuaded not to by other Liberals. Only in 1893 did Laurier become encouraged again. On June 20 and 21, 1893, Laurier convened a Liberal convention in Ottawa. The convention established that unrestricted reciprocity was intended to develop Canada's natural resources and that keeping a customs tariff was intended to generate revenue. Laurier subsequently undertook a series of speaking tours to campaign on the convention's results. Laurier visited <a href="/wiki/Western_Canada" title="Western Canada">Western Canada</a> in September and October 1894, promising to relax the Conservatives' <a href="/wiki/National_Policy" title="National Policy">National Policy</a>, open the American market, and increase <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada" title="Immigration to Canada">immigration</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Macdonald died only three months after he defeated Laurier in the 1891 election. After Macdonald's death, the Conservatives went through a period of disorganization with four short-serving leaders. The fourth prime minister after Macdonald, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Tupper" title="Charles Tupper">Charles Tupper</a>, became prime minister in May 1896 after <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_Bowell" title="Mackenzie Bowell">Mackenzie Bowell</a> resigned as a result of a leadership crisis that was triggered by his attempts to offer a compromise for the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question" title="Manitoba Schools Question">Manitoba Schools Question</a>, a dispute which emerged after the provincial government ended funding for Catholic schools in 1890. Tupper faced Laurier in the <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896 federal election</a>, in which the schools dispute was a key issue. While Tupper supported overriding the provincial legislation to reinstate funding for the Catholic schools, Laurier was vague when giving his position on the matter, proposing an investigation of the issue first and then conciliation, a method he famously called, "sunny ways". On June 23, Laurier led the Liberals to their first victory in 22 years, despite losing the popular vote. Laurier's win was made possible by his sweep in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281896.E2.80.931911.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1896–1911)">Prime Minister (1896–1911)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1896–1911)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg/171px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg" decoding="async" width="171" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg/257px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg/343px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_c._1897.jpg 2x" data-file-width="439" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Laurier, 1897</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policy">Domestic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Domestic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Manitoba_Schools_Question">Manitoba Schools Question</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Manitoba Schools Question">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>One of Laurier's first acts as prime minister was to implement a solution to the Manitoba Schools Question, which had helped to bring down the Conservative government of Charles Tupper earlier in 1896. The Manitoba legislature had passed a law eliminating public funding for Catholic schooling. Supporters of Catholic schools argued that the new statute was contrary to the provisions of the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Act,_1870" title="Manitoba Act, 1870">Manitoba Act, 1870</a>, which had a provision relating to school funding, but the courts rejected that argument and held that the new statute was constitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The Catholic minority in Manitoba then asked the federal government for support, and eventually, the Conservatives proposed remedial legislation to override Manitoba's legislation. Laurier opposed the remedial legislation on the basis of provincial rights and succeeded in blocking its passage by Parliament. Once elected, Laurier reached a compromise with the provincial premier, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Greenway" title="Thomas Greenway">Thomas Greenway</a>. Known as the Laurier-Greenway Compromise, the agreement did not allow separate Catholic schools to be re-established. However, religious instruction (Catholic education) would take place for 30 minutes at the end of each day, if requested by the parents of 10 children in rural areas or 25 in urban areas. Catholic teachers were allowed to be hired in the schools as long as there were at least 40 Catholic students in urban areas or 25 Catholic students in rural areas, and teachers could speak in French (or any other minority language) as long as there were enough Francophone students. This was seen by many as the best possible solution in the circumstances, however, some French Canadians criticized this move as it was done on an individual basis, and did not protect Catholic or French rights in all schools. Laurier called his effort to lessen the tinder in this issue "sunny ways" (French: <i lang="fr">voies ensoleillées</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Railway_construction">Railway construction</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Railway construction">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Laurier's government introduced and initiated the idea of constructing a second <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_railway" class="mw-redirect" title="Transcontinental railway">transcontinental railway</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Pacific_Railway" title="Grand Trunk Pacific Railway">Grand Trunk Pacific Railway</a>. The first transcontinental railway, the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Railway" title="Canadian Pacific Railway">Canadian Pacific Railway</a>, had limitations and was not able to meet everyone's needs. In <a href="/wiki/Western_Canada" title="Western Canada">the West</a>, the railway was not able to transport everything produced by farmers and in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Canada" title="Eastern Canada">the East</a>, the railway did not reach into <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ontario" title="Northern Ontario">Northern Ontario</a> and <a href="/wiki/Northern_Quebec" title="Northern Quebec">Northern Quebec</a>. Laurier was in favour of a transcontinental line built entirely on Canadian land by private enterprise.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Laurier_automobile2.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Laurier_automobile2.png/220px-Laurier_automobile2.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Laurier_automobile2.png/330px-Laurier_automobile2.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Laurier_automobile2.png/440px-Laurier_automobile2.png 2x" data-file-width="1268" data-file-height="952" /></a><figcaption>Laurier (middle) on a chauffeur-driven automobile</figcaption></figure>
<p>Laurier's government also constructed a third railway: the <a href="/wiki/National_Transcontinental_Railway" title="National Transcontinental Railway">National Transcontinental Railway</a>. It was made to provide Western Canada with direct rail connection to the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Canada" title="Atlantic Canada">Atlantic</a> ports and to open up and develop Northern Ontario and Northern Quebec. Laurier believed that competition between the three railways would force one of the three, the Canadian Pacific Railway, to lower <a href="/wiki/Freight_rate" title="Freight rate">freight rates</a> and thus please Western shippers who would contribute to the competition between the railways. Laurier initially reached out to <a href="/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Railway" title="Grand Trunk Railway">Grand Trunk Railway</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Northern_Railway" title="Canadian Northern Railway">Canadian Northern Railway</a> to build the National Transcontinental railway, but after disagreements emerged between the two companies, Laurier's government opted to build part of the railway itself. However, Laurier's government soon struck a deal with the Grand Trunk Pacific Railway Company (subsidiary of the Grand Trunk Railway Company) to build the western section (from <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a> to the Pacific Ocean) while the government would build the eastern section (from Winnipeg to <a href="/wiki/Moncton" title="Moncton">Moncton</a>). Once completed, Laurier's government would hand over the railway to the company for operation. Laurier's government gained criticism from the public due to the heavy cost to construct the railway.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Provincial_and_territorial_boundaries">Provincial and territorial boundaries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Provincial and territorial boundaries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On September 1, 1905, through the <i><a href="/wiki/Alberta_Act" title="Alberta Act">Alberta Act</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Saskatchewan_Act" title="Saskatchewan Act">Saskatchewan Act</a></i>, Laurier oversaw <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saskatchewan" title="Saskatchewan">Saskatchewan</a>'s entry into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>, the last two provinces to be created out of the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">Northwest Territories</a>. Laurier decided to create two provinces, arguing that one large province would be too difficult to govern.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> This followed the enactment of the <i>Yukon Territory Act</i> by the Laurier Government in 1898, separating the <a href="/wiki/Yukon" title="Yukon">Yukon</a> from the Northwest Territories.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Also in 1898, Quebec was enlarged through the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Boundary_Extension_Act,_1898" title="Quebec Boundary Extension Act, 1898"><i>Quebec Boundary Extension Act</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Immigration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Laurier's government dramatically increased immigration to grow the economy. Between 1897 and 1914, at least a million immigrants arrived in Canada, and Canada's population increased by 40 percent. Laurier's immigration policy targeted the <a href="/wiki/Prairies" class="mw-redirect" title="Prairies">Prairies</a> as he argued that it would increase farming production and benefit the <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_Canada" title="Agriculture in Canada">agriculture industry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British Columbia electorate was alarmed at the arrival of people they considered "uncivilized" by Canadian standards, and adopted a whites-only policy. Although railways and large companies wanted to hire Asians, labour unions and the public at large stood opposed.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>  Both major parties went along with public opinion, with Laurier taking the lead.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Scholars have argued that Laurier acted in terms of his racist views in restricting immigration from China and India, as shown by his support for the Chinese <a href="/wiki/Head_tax_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Head tax (Canada)">head tax</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In 1900, Laurier raised the Chinese head tax to $100.  In 1903, this was further raised to $500,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> but when a few Chinese did pay the $500, he proposed raising the sum to $1,000.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> This was not the first time Laurier showed racially charged action, and over the course of his time as a politician, he had a history of racist views and actions.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In 1886, Laurier told the House of Commons that it was moral for Canada to take lands from “savage nations” so long as the government paid adequate compensation.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Laurier also negotiated a limit to Japanese emigration to Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1911, Laurier approved the <a href="/wiki/Order-in-Council_P.C._1911-1324" title="Order-in-Council P.C. 1911-1324">Order-in-Council P.C. 1911-1324</a> recommended by the <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_the_Interior_(Canada)" title="Minister of the Interior (Canada)">minister of the interior</a>, <a href="/wiki/Frank_Oliver_(politician)" title="Frank Oliver (politician)">Frank Oliver</a>. The order was approved by the cabinet on August 12, 1911. The order was intended to keep out Black Americans escaping segregation in the American south, stating that "the Negro race...is deemed unsuitable to the climate and requirements of Canada." The order was never called upon, as efforts by immigration officials had already reduced the number of Blacks migrating to Canada. The order was cancelled on October 5, 1911, the day before Laurier left office, by cabinet claiming that the minister of the interior was not present at the time of approval.<sup id="cite_ref-ce_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ce-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg/220px-SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg/330px-SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg/440px-SirAndLadyLaurier.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2349" data-file-height="1659" /></a><figcaption>Sir Wilfrid Laurier with <a href="/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Laurier" title="Zoé Laurier">Zoé, Lady Laurier</a>, in 1907</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_policy">Social policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Social policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In March 1906, Laurier's government introduced the <i>Lord's Day Act</i> after being persuaded by the Lord's Day Alliance. The Act became effective on March 1, 1907. It prohibited business transactions from taking place on Sundays; it also restricted Sunday trade, labour, recreation, and newspapers. The Act was supported by organized labour and the French Canadian Catholic hierarchy but was opposed by those who worked in the manufacturing and transportation sectors. It was also opposed by French Canadians due to them believing the federal government was interfering in a provincial matter; the Quebec government passed its own <i>Lord’s Day Act</i> that came into effect one day before the federal act did.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1907, Laurier's government passed the <i>Industrial Disputes Investigation Act</i>, which mandated conciliation for employers and workers before any strike in public utilities or mines, but did not make it necessary for the groups to accept the conciliators’ report.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In 1908, a system was introduced where by annuities may be purchased from the government, the aim of which was to encourage voluntary provision for old age.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg/250px-1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg/375px-1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg/500px-1902_Colonial_Conference.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2320" data-file-height="1544" /></a><figcaption>Laurier (seated third from the left) at the <a href="/wiki/1902_Colonial_Conference" title="1902 Colonial Conference">1902 Colonial Conference</a></figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="United_Kingdom">United Kingdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: United Kingdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On June 22, 1897, Laurier attended the <a href="/wiki/Diamond_Jubilee_of_Queen_Victoria" title="Diamond Jubilee of Queen Victoria">Diamond Jubilee of Queen Victoria</a>, which was the 60th anniversary of her coronation. There, he was knighted, and was given several honours, honorary degrees, and medals.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Laurier again visited the United Kingdom in 1902, taking part in the <a href="/wiki/1902_Colonial_Conference" title="1902 Colonial Conference">1902 Colonial Conference</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_King_Edward_VII_and_Queen_Alexandra" class="mw-redirect" title="Coronation of King Edward VII and Queen Alexandra">coronation</a> of King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Edward VII</a> on August 9, 1902. Laurier also took part in the <a href="/wiki/1907_Imperial_Conference" title="1907 Imperial Conference">1907</a> and <a href="/wiki/1911_Imperial_Conference" title="1911 Imperial Conference">1911</a> Imperial Conferences.<sup id="cite_ref-WLBritannica_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLBritannica-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1899, the United Kingdom expected military support from Canada, as part of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a>. Laurier was caught between demands for support for military action from English Canada and a strong opposition from French Canada, which saw the Boer War as an English war. Laurier eventually decided to send a volunteer force, rather than the <a href="/wiki/Militia" title="Militia">militia</a> expected by <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">Britain</a>. Roughly 7,000 Canadian soldiers participated in the volunteer force. Outspoken <a href="/wiki/Quebec_nationalism" title="Quebec nationalism">French Canadian nationalist</a> and Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Henri_Bourassa" title="Henri Bourassa">Henri Bourassa</a> was an especially vocal opponent of any form of military participation and thus resigned from the Liberal caucus in October 1899.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On June 1, 1909, Laurier's government established the <a href="/wiki/Global_Affairs_Canada" title="Global Affairs Canada">Department of External Affairs</a> for Canada to take greater control of its foreign policy.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The naval competition between the United Kingdom and the <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">German Empire</a> escalated in the early years of the 20th century. The British asked Canada for more money and resources for ship construction, precipitating a heated political division in Canada. Many English Canadians wished to send as much as possible; many French Canadians and those against wished to send nothing. Aiming for compromise, Laurier advanced the <i><a href="/wiki/Naval_Service_Act" title="Naval Service Act">Naval Service Act</a></i> of 1910 which created the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Navy" title="Royal Canadian Navy">Royal Canadian Navy</a>. The navy would initially consist of five <a href="/wiki/Cruiser" title="Cruiser">cruisers</a> and six <a href="/wiki/Destroyer" title="Destroyer">destroyers</a>; in times of crisis, it could be made subordinate to the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">British Royal Navy</a>. However, the idea faced opposition in both English and French Canada, especially in Quebec as ex-Liberal and staunch French Canadian nationalist Henri Bourassa organized an anti-Laurier force.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Alaska_boundary_dispute">Alaska boundary dispute</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Alaska boundary dispute">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1897 and 1898, the <a href="/wiki/Alaska" title="Alaska">Alaska</a>-Canada border emerged as a pressing issue. The <a href="/wiki/Klondike_Gold_Rush" title="Klondike Gold Rush">Klondike Gold Rush</a> prompted Laurier to demand an all-Canadian route from the gold fields to a seaport. The region being a desirable place with lots of gold furthered Laurier's ambition of fixing an exact boundary. Laurier also wanted to establish who owned the <a href="/wiki/Lynn_Canal" title="Lynn Canal">Lynn Canal</a> and who controlled maritime access to the Yukon. Laurier and US President <a href="/wiki/William_McKinley" title="William McKinley">William McKinley</a> agreed to set up a joint Anglo-American commission that would study the differences and resolve <a href="/wiki/Alaska_boundary_dispute" title="Alaska boundary dispute">the dispute</a>. However, this commission was unsuccessful and came to an abrupt end on February 20, 1899.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The dispute was then referred to an international judicial commission in 1903, which included three American politicians (<a href="/wiki/Elihu_Root" title="Elihu Root">Elihu Root</a>, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge" title="Henry Cabot Lodge">Henry Cabot Lodge</a>, and <a href="/wiki/George_Turner_(American_politician)" title="George Turner (American politician)">George Turner</a>), two Canadians (<a href="/wiki/Allen_Bristol_Aylesworth" title="Allen Bristol Aylesworth">Allen Bristol Aylesworth</a> and <a href="/wiki/Louis-Amable_Jett%C3%A9" title="Louis-Amable Jetté">Louis-Amable Jetté</a>) and one Briton (<a href="/wiki/Lord_Alverstone" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Alverstone">Lord Alverstone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chief_Justice_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Chief Justice of England">Lord Chief Justice of England</a>). On October 20, 1903, the commission by a majority (Root, Lodge, Turner, and Alverstone) ruled to support the American government's claims. Canada only acquired two islands below the <a href="/wiki/Portland_Canal" title="Portland Canal">Portland Canal</a>. The decision provoked a wave of anti-American and anti-British sentiment in Canada, which Laurier temporarily encouraged.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tariffs_and_trade">Tariffs and trade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Tariffs and trade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Though supportive of <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> with the United States, Laurier did not pursue the idea because the American government refused to discuss the issue. Instead, he implemented a Liberal version of the Conservatives' nationalist and protectionist <a href="/wiki/National_Policy" title="National Policy">National Policy</a> by maintaining high tariffs on goods from other countries that restricted Canadian goods. However, he lowered tariffs to the same level as countries that admitted Canadian goods.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1897, Laurier's government impelemented a preferential reduction of a tariff rate of 12.5 percent for countries that imported Canadian goods at a rate equivalent to the minimum Canadian charge; rates for countries that imposed a protective duty against Canada remained the same. For the most part, the policy was supported by those for free trade (due to the preferential reduction) and those against free trade (due to elements of the National Policy remaining in place).<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier's government again reformed tariffs in 1907. His government introduced a "three-column tariff", which added a new intermediate rate (a bargaining rate) alongside the existing British preferential rate and the general rate (which applied to all countries that Canada had no most-favoured-nation agreement with). The preferential and general rates remained unchanged, while the intermediate rates were slightly lower than the general rates.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Also in 1907, Laurier's <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">minister of finance</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Stevens_Fielding" title="William Stevens Fielding">William Stevens Fielding</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Ministers_of_Fisheries,_Oceans_and_the_Canadian_Coast_Guard" class="mw-redirect" title="Ministers of Fisheries, Oceans and the Canadian Coast Guard">minister of marine and fisheries</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louis-Philippe_Brodeur" title="Louis-Philippe Brodeur">Louis-Philippe Brodeur</a>, negotiated a trade agreement with France which lowered import duties on some goods. In 1909, Fielding negotiated an agreement to promote trade with the <a href="/wiki/British_West_Indies" title="British West Indies">British West Indies</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fielding_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fielding-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_victories">Election victories</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Election victories">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Laurier led the Liberals to three re-elections in <a href="/wiki/1900_Canadian_federal_election" title="1900 Canadian federal election">1900</a>, <a href="/wiki/1904_Canadian_federal_election" title="1904 Canadian federal election">1904</a>, and <a href="/wiki/1908_Canadian_federal_election" title="1908 Canadian federal election">1908</a>. In the 1900 and 1904 elections, the Liberals' popular vote and seat share kept increasing whereas in the 1908 election, their popular vote and seat share went slightly down.
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg/175px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg/263px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg/350px-Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_durant_son_dernier_voyage_dans_l%27Ouest_canadien.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="632" /></a><figcaption>Laurier, towards the end of his tenure</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Quebec_stronghold">Quebec stronghold</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Quebec stronghold">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>By the late 1900s, Laurier had been able to build the Liberal Party a base in Quebec, which had remained a Conservative stronghold for decades due to the province's social conservatism and to the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>, which distrusted the Liberals' <a href="/wiki/Anti-clericalism" title="Anti-clericalism">anti-clericalism</a>. The growing alienation of <a href="/wiki/French_Canadians" title="French Canadians">French Canadians</a> from the Conservative Party due to its links with anti-French, anti-Catholic <a href="/wiki/Orange_Institution" class="mw-redirect" title="Orange Institution">Orangemen</a> in English Canada aided the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> These factors, combined with the collapse of the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Quebec_(historical)" title="Conservative Party of Quebec (historical)">Conservative Party of Quebec</a>, gave Laurier an opportunity to build a stronghold in French Canada and among Catholics across Canada. However, Catholic priests in Quebec repeatedly warned their parishioners not to vote for Liberals. Their slogan was "<span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">le ciel est bleu, l'enfer est rouge</i></span>" ("heaven is blue, hell is red", referring to the Conservative and Liberal parties' traditional colours).<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reciprocity_and_defeat">Reciprocity and defeat</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Reciprocity and defeat">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1911, controversy arose regarding Laurier's support of trade <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(Canadian_politics)" title="Reciprocity (Canadian politics)">reciprocity</a> with the United States. His long-serving minister of finance, <a href="/wiki/William_Stevens_Fielding" title="William Stevens Fielding">William Stevens Fielding</a>, reached an agreement allowing for the free trade of natural products. The agreement would also lower <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a>. This had the strong support of agricultural interests, particularly in Western Canada, but it alienated many businessmen who formed a significant part of the Liberal base. The <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historical)">Conservatives</a> denounced the deal and played on long-standing fears that reciprocity could eventually lead to weakened ties with Britain and a Canadian economy dominated by the United States. They also campaigned on fears that this would lead to the Canadian identity being taken away by the US and the American annexation of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Contending with an unruly House of Commons, including vocal disapproval from Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Clifford_Sifton" title="Clifford Sifton">Clifford Sifton</a>, Laurier called <a href="/wiki/1911_Canadian_federal_election" title="1911 Canadian federal election">an election</a> to settle the issue of reciprocity. The Conservatives were victorious and the Liberals lost over a third of their seats. The Conservatives' leader, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Laird_Borden" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Laird Borden">Robert Laird Borden</a>, succeeded Laurier as prime minister. Over 15 consecutive years of Liberal rule ended.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_and_war_.281911.E2.80.931919.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_and_war_(1911–1919)">Opposition and war (1911–1919)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Opposition and war (1911–1919)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg/200px-Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg/300px-Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg/400px-Affiche_%C3%A9lectorale_de_Wilfrid_Laurier.jpg 2x" data-file-width="464" data-file-height="639" /></a><figcaption>Election flyer for Wilfrid Laurier and the Liberal Party in the <a href="/wiki/1917_Canadian_federal_election" title="1917 Canadian federal election">1917 federal election</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Laurier stayed on as Liberal leader. In December 1912, he started leading the filibuster and fight against the Conservatives' own naval bill which would have sent $35 million directly to the British Navy. Laurier argued that this threatened Canadian autonomy. After six months of battling the bill, the bill was blocked by the Liberal-controlled <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier led the opposition during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>. He supported sending a volunteer force to fight in the war, arguing that an intense campaign for volunteers would produce enough troops. Borden initially had a volunteer military system in place, but when applications started to decline, he imposed <a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a> in the summer of 1917, which led to the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a>. Laurier was an influential opponent of <a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a>, and his position on the matter was applauded by French Canadians, who were generally anti-conscription. Pro-conscription Liberals, particularly from English Canada, joined Borden as <a href="/wiki/Liberal-Unionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal-Unionist">Liberal-Unionists</a> to form the <a href="/wiki/Unionist_Party_(Canada)" title="Unionist Party (Canada)">Union government</a>. Laurier refused to join the Unionist Party, and instead created the "<a href="/wiki/Laurier_Liberals" title="Laurier Liberals">Laurier Liberals</a>", a party composed of Liberals opposed to conscription. Laurier also rejected Prime Minister Borden's proposal to form a <a href="/wiki/Coalition_government" title="Coalition government">coalition government</a> composed of both Conservatives and Liberals, arguing that there would be no "real" opposition to the government. He also argued that if the Liberals joined, Quebec would feel alienated and would lead to the province being heavily influenced by outspoken French-Canadian nationalist <a href="/wiki/Henri_Bourassa" title="Henri Bourassa">Henri Bourassa</a>, and what Laurier called Bourassa's "dangerous nationalism" which might lead to Quebec seceding from Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1917_Canadian_federal_election" title="1917 Canadian federal election">1917 election</a>, the Laurier Liberals were reduced to a mostly French Canadian <a href="/wiki/Rump_party" title="Rump party">rump</a>. Laurier swept Quebec, winning 62 out of 65 of the province's seats, not least due to the French Canadians' overwhelming respect and support for Laurier as a result of his opposition to conscription.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wilfrid_Laurier_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilfrid_Laurier-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Conscription Crisis once again revealed the divisions between French Canadians and English Canadians. Most English Canadians favoured conscription as they believed this would strengthen ties with the British Empire, whereas most French Canadians opposed conscription as they wanted nothing to do with the war. Laurier was now seen as a "traitor" to English Canadians and English Canadian Liberals, whereas he was seen as a "hero" for French Canadians. Laurier's protégé and successor as party leader, <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a>, unified the English and French factions of the Liberal Party, leading it to victory over the Conservatives in the <a href="/wiki/1921_Canadian_federal_election" title="1921 Canadian federal election">1921 federal election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the election, Laurier still stayed on as Liberal and Opposition leader. When World War I came to an end on November 11, 1918, he focused on his efforts to rebuild and reunify the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wilfrid_Laurier_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilfrid_Laurier-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Laurier died of a stroke on February 17, 1919, while still in office as leader of the Opposition. Though he had lost a bitter election two years earlier, he was loved nationwide for his "warm smile, his sense of style, and his "sunny ways"."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> 50,000 to 100,000 people jammed the streets of Ottawa as his funeral procession marched to his final resting place at <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame_Cemetery" class="mw-redirect" title="Notre Dame Cemetery">Notre Dame Cemetery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> His remains would eventually be placed in a stone sarcophagus, adorned by sculptures of nine mourning female figures, representing each of the provinces in the union. His wife, <a href="/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Laurier" title="Zoé Laurier">Zoé Laurier</a>, died on November 1, 1921 and was placed in the same tomb.
</p><p>Laurier <a href="/wiki/1919_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1919 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">was permanently succeeded as Liberal leader</a> by his former <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(Canada)" title="Minister of Labour (Canada)">minister of labour</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a>. King narrowly defeated Laurier's former <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">minister of finance</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Stevens_Fielding" title="William Stevens Fielding">William Stevens Fielding</a>. According to Zoé, Fielding was Laurier's choice for next leader; Laurier believed Fielding had the best chance to restore unity in the party.<sup id="cite_ref-Fielding_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fielding-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg/150px-Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="198" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg/225px-Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg/300px-Lady_Laurier_photo_by_William_James_Topley_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="429" data-file-height="566" /></a><figcaption>Zoé, Lady Laurier</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilfrid Laurier married <a href="/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Lafontaine" class="mw-redirect" title="Zoé Lafontaine">Zoé Lafontaine</a> in Montreal on May 13, 1868. She was the daughter of G.N.R. Lafontaine and his first wife, Zoé Tessier known as Zoé Lavigne.
