Laurier's wife Zoé was born in Montreal and educated there at the School of the Bon Pasteur, and at the Convent of the Sisters of the Sacred Heart, St. Vincent de Paul. The couple lived at Arthabaskaville until they moved to Ottawa in 1896. She served as one of the vice presidents on the formation of the National Council of Women and was honorary vice president of the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Order_of_Nurses" title="Victorian Order of Nurses">Victorian Order of Nurses</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The couple had no children.
</p><p>
Beginning in 1878 and for some twenty years while married to Zoé, Laurier had an "ambiguous relationship" with a married woman, <a href="/wiki/%C3%89milie_Lavergne" class="mw-redirect" title="Émilie Lavergne">Émilie Barthe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Zoé was not an intellectual; Émilie was, and relished literature and politics like Wilfrid, whose heart she won. Rumour had it he fathered a son, <a href="/wiki/Armand_Lavergne" title="Armand Lavergne">Armand Lavergne</a>, with her, yet Zoé remained with him until his death.<sup id="cite_ref-WLbio_1-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLbio-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg/175px-Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg/263px-Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg/350px-Laurier_monument_Feb_2005.jpg 2x" data-file-width="723" data-file-height="966" /></a><figcaption>Wilfrid Laurier's grave, sculpted by <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Lalibert%C3%A9" title="Alfred Laliberté">Alfred Laliberté</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame_Cemetery" class="mw-redirect" title="Notre Dame Cemetery">Notre Dame Cemetery</a>, Ottawa</figcaption></figure>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Overall, Laurier's efforts to remain neutral between English Canadians and French Canadians, and his efforts to seek a middle ground between the two ethnic groups have paved the way for him to be <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">ranked</a> among the top three of Canadian prime ministers. Despite being a French Canadian, he did not fully accept the French Canadian demands of repealing Manitoba's ban on public funding for Catholic schools nor did he fully accept their demands of refusing to send any Canadian troop to fight in the Boer War. Nonetheless, in all seven elections he fought, the majority of Quebec's ridings were handed over to his Liberal Party. Despite one notable exception in <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958</a>, the Liberal Party continued to dominate federal politics in Quebec until <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984</a>.
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Monet" title="Jacques Monet">Jacques Monet</a> wrote, "To his faithful followers, especially in Quebec, where his surname is used as a first name by many other Canadians, Laurier is a charismatic hero whose term of office was a happy time in Canadian history. He worked all his life for cooperation between French- and English-speaking Canadians while he strove to keep Canada as independent as possible from Britain. His personal charm and dignity, his great skill as an orator, and his great gifts of intellect won the admiration of all Canadians and non-Canadians alike."<sup id="cite_ref-WLBritannica_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WLBritannica-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to historians <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a> and Stephen Azzi, a 2011 poll of 117 historians and experts voted Laurier as the "best" Canadian prime minister, ahead of <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">Mackenzie King</a>. Laurier was ranked Number 3 of the Prime Ministers of Canada (out of the 20 through <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>) in the survey by Canadian historians included in <i>Prime Ministers: Ranking Canada's Leaders</i> by <a href="/wiki/J.L._Granatstein" class="mw-redirect" title="J.L. Granatstein">J.L. Granatstein</a> and Norman Hillmer.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> "Passionate, charismatic, and an intellectual force in both languages," the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_War_Museum" title="Canadian War Museum">Canadian War Museum</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Tim_Cook_(historian)" title="Tim Cook (historian)">Tim Cook</a> stated, "Sir Wilfrid was the full package."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Recognition">Recognition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Recognition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_historic_sites">National historic sites</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: National historic sites">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MaisonLaurier.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/MaisonLaurier.jpg/170px-MaisonLaurier.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="251" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/MaisonLaurier.jpg/255px-MaisonLaurier.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/MaisonLaurier.jpg/340px-MaisonLaurier.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1422" data-file-height="2102" /></a><figcaption>Laurier Museum, Victoriaville, QC</figcaption></figure>
<p>Laurier is commemorated by three <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Sites_of_Canada" title="National Historic Sites of Canada">National Historic Sites</a>.
</p><p>The Sir Wilfrid Laurier National Historic Site is in his birthplace, <a href="/wiki/Saint-Lin,_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint-Lin, Quebec">Saint-Lin-Laurentides</a>, a town 60&#160;km (37&#160;mi) north of <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, Quebec. Its establishment reflected an early desire to not only mark his birthplace (a plaque in 1925 and a monument in 1927), but to create a shrine to Laurier in the 1930s. Despite early doubts and later confirmation that the house designated as the birthplace was neither Laurier's nor on its original site, its development, and the building of a museum, satisfied the goal of honoring the man and reflecting his early life.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Laurier's brick residence in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> is known as <a href="/wiki/Laurier_House" title="Laurier House">Laurier House</a> National Historic Site, at the corner of what is now Laurier Avenue and Chapel Street. In their will, the Lauriers left the house to Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">Mackenzie King</a>, who in turn donated it to Canada upon his death. Both sites are administered by <a href="/wiki/Parks_Canada" title="Parks Canada">Parks Canada</a> as part of the national park system.
</p><p>The 1876 Italianate residence of the Lauriers during his years as a lawyer and Member of Parliament, in Victoriaville, Quebec, is designated Wilfrid Laurier House National Historic Site, owned privately and operated as the Laurier Museum.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2011, Wilfrid Laurier University located in Waterloo, Ontario, unveiled a statue depicting a young Wilfrid Laurier sitting on a bench, thinking.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_honours">Other honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Other honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Laurier had titular honours including:
</p>
<ul><li>the prenominal "<a href="/wiki/The_Honourable" title="The Honourable">The Honourable</a>" and the postnominal "PC" for life by virtue of being made a member of the <a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">Queen's Privy Council for Canada</a> on October 8, 1877.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>His prenominal was upgraded to "<a href="/wiki/The_Right_Honourable" title="The Right Honourable">The Right Honourable</a>" when he was made a member of the Imperial <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council of the United Kingdom</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1897_Diamond_Jubilee_Honours" title="1897 Diamond Jubilee Honours">1897 Diamond Jubilee Honours</a></li>
<li>the prenominal "Sir" and postnominal "GCMG" as a knight grand cross of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Michael_and_Saint_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Michael and Saint George">Order of Saint Michael and Saint George</a>, bestowed in the 1897 Diamond Jubilee Honours</li>
<li>The honorary degree <a href="/wiki/LL.D." class="mw-redirect" title="LL.D.">LL.D.</a> from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">University of Edinburgh</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_City" title="Freedom of the City">Freedom of the City</a> of <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a> on July 26, 1902, when he visited the city while in the country for the coronation of King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Edward VII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><b>Sir Wilfrid Laurier Day</b> is observed each year on November 20, his birth date<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Laurier is depicted on several banknotes issued by the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Canada" title="Bank of Canada">Bank of Canada</a>:</li></ul>
<dl><dd><dl><dd>The $1,000 note in the <a href="/wiki/1935_Series_(banknotes)" class="mw-redirect" title="1935 Series (banknotes)">1935 Series</a> and <a href="/wiki/1937_Series_(banknotes)" class="mw-redirect" title="1937 Series (banknotes)">1937 Series</a></dd>
<dd>The $5 note in the <a href="/wiki/Scenes_of_Canada_(banknotes)" class="mw-redirect" title="Scenes of Canada (banknotes)">Scenes of Canada</a> series, 1972 and 1979, <a href="/wiki/Banknotes_of_the_Canadian_dollar" title="Banknotes of the Canadian dollar">Birds of Canada</a> series, 1986, <a href="/wiki/Journey_Series_(banknotes)" class="mw-redirect" title="Journey Series (banknotes)">Journey series</a>, 2002 and <a href="/wiki/Banknotes_of_the_Canadian_dollar" title="Banknotes of the Canadian dollar">Frontier</a> series, 2013</dd></dl></dd></dl>
<ul><li>Laurier has appeared on at least three postage stamps, issued in 1927 (two) and 1973</li></ul>

//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Laurier_statue%2C_Ottawa.jpg/400px-Laurier_statue%2C_Ottawa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1249" data-file-height="1459" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Joseph-%C3%89mile_Brunet" title="Joseph-Émile Brunet">Joseph-Émile Brunet</a>'s statue of Wilfrid Laurier behind the <a href="/wiki/East_Block" title="East Block">East Block</a> on <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Many sites and landmarks were named to honor Laurier. They include:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mount_Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Mount Sir Wilfrid Laurier">Mount Sir Wilfrid Laurier</a>, the highest peak in <a href="/wiki/British_Columbia" title="British Columbia">British Columbia</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Premier_Range" title="Premier Range">Premier Range</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Mount_Robson" title="Mount Robson">Mount Robson</a></li>
<li>Sir Wilfrid Laurier Elementary, located in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver,_British_Columbia" class="mw-redirect" title="Vancouver, British Columbia">Vancouver, British Columbia</a></li>
<li>Laurier Avenue, located in <a href="/wiki/Milton,_Ontario" title="Milton, Ontario">Milton, Ontario</a></li>
<li>Avenue Laurier, located in <a href="/wiki/Shawinigan" title="Shawinigan">Shawinigan</a>, Quebec</li>
<li>Laurier Boulevard, and Laurier Hill, located in <a href="/wiki/Brockville" title="Brockville">Brockville</a>, Ontario</li>
<li>Avenue Laurier, located in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, Quebec</li>
<li>Boulevard Laurier, located in <a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>, Quebec</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Laurier_Avenue" title="Laurier Avenue">Laurier Avenue</a>, located in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>, Ontario</li>
<li>Laurier Avenue, located in <a href="/wiki/Deep_River,_Ontario" title="Deep River, Ontario">Deep River, Ontario</a></li>
<li>Laurier Street, located in <a href="/wiki/North_Bay,_Ontario" title="North Bay, Ontario">North Bay, Ontario</a></li>
<li>Rue Laurier, located in <a href="/wiki/Casselman,_Ontario" title="Casselman, Ontario">Casselman, Ontario</a></li>
<li>Rue Laurier Street, located in Rockland, Ontario</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Laurier_Heights,_Edmonton" title="Laurier Heights, Edmonton">Laurier Heights neighbourhood</a>, including Laurier Drive and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Laurier_Heights_School&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Laurier Heights School (page does not exist)">Laurier Heights School</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Edmonton" title="Edmonton">Edmonton</a>, Alberta</li>
<li>Laurier Drive, located in <a href="/wiki/Saskatoon" title="Saskatoon">Saskatoon</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Confederation_Park,_Saskatoon" title="Confederation Park, Saskatoon">Confederation Park</a> neighbourhood, where the majority of the streets are named after former Canadian prime ministers</li>
<li>The provincial electoral district of <a href="/wiki/Laurier-Dorion" title="Laurier-Dorion">Laurier-Dorion</a> (an honour shared with Canadian politician <a href="/wiki/Antoine-Aim%C3%A9_Dorion" title="Antoine-Aimé Dorion">Antoine-Aimé Dorion</a>)</li>
<li>The federal electoral district of <a href="/wiki/Laurier%E2%80%94Sainte-Marie" title="Laurier—Sainte-Marie">Laurier—Sainte-Marie</a></li>
<li>On November 1, 1973, Waterloo Lutheran University, one of Ontario's publicly funded universities, located in <a href="/wiki/Waterloo,_Ontario" title="Waterloo, Ontario">Waterloo, Ontario</a>, was renamed <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier_University" title="Wilfrid Laurier University">Wilfrid Laurier University</a>; the university has since added campuses in <a href="/wiki/Brantford,_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Brantford, Ontario">Brantford, Ontario</a> and <a href="/wiki/Milton,_Ontario" title="Milton, Ontario">Milton, Ontario</a></li>
<li>A <a href="/wiki/Montreal_Metro" title="Montreal Metro">Montreal Metro</a> station, <a href="/wiki/Laurier_(Montreal_Metro)" class="mw-redirect" title="Laurier (Montreal Metro)">Laurier (Montreal Metro)</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/CCGS_Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier" title="CCGS Sir Wilfrid Laurier">CCGS <i>Sir Wilfrid Laurier</i></a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_Laurier" title="Château Laurier">Château Laurier</a>, a downtown Ottawa hotel of high reputation and a national historic site</li>
<li>Sir Wilfrid Laurier Public School in <a href="/wiki/Markham,_Ontario" title="Markham, Ontario">Markham, Ontario</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_School_Board" title="Sir Wilfrid Laurier School Board">Sir Wilfrid Laurier School Board</a>, an English school board located in <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>; the school board serves the <a href="/wiki/Laval,_Quebec" title="Laval, Quebec">Laval</a>, <a href="/wiki/Laurentides" title="Laurentides">Laurentides</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lanaudi%C3%A8re" title="Lanaudière">Lanaudière</a> regions in Quebec</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_Secondary_School_(London,_Ontario)" title="Sir Wilfrid Laurier Secondary School (London, Ontario)">Sir Wilfrid Laurier Secondary School</a> in <a href="/wiki/London,_Ontario" title="London, Ontario">London, Ontario</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_Secondary_School_(Ottawa)" title="Sir Wilfrid Laurier Secondary School (Ottawa)">Sir Wilfrid Laurier Secondary School</a> in Ottawa, Ontario</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Wilfrid_Laurier_Collegiate_Institute" title="Sir Wilfrid Laurier Collegiate Institute">Sir Wilfrid Laurier Collegiate Institute</a> in <a href="/wiki/Scarborough,_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="Scarborough, Toronto">Scarborough</a>, Ontario</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Wilfrid Laurier advised the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> to appoint the following individuals to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a>:
</p>
<ul><li>Sir <a href="/wiki/Louis_Henry_Davies" title="Louis Henry Davies">Louis Henry Davies</a> (September 25, 1901 – May 1, 1924)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/David_Mills_(Canadian_politician)" title="David Mills (Canadian politician)">David Mills</a> (February 8, 1902 – May 8, 1903)</li>
<li>Sir <a href="/wiki/Henri_Elz%C3%A9ar_Taschereau" class="mw-redirect" title="Henri Elzéar Taschereau">Henri Elzéar Taschereau</a> (as Chief Justice November 21, 1902 – May 2, 1906; appointed a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_Justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne Justice">Puisne Justice</a> under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Mackenzie_(politician)" title="Alexander Mackenzie (politician)">Mackenzie</a>, October 7, 1878)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Douglas_Armour" title="John Douglas Armour">John Douglas Armour</a> (November 21, 1902 – July 11, 1903)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Wallace_Nesbitt" title="Wallace Nesbitt">Wallace Nesbitt</a> (May 16, 1903 – October 4, 1905)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Albert_Clements_Killam" title="Albert Clements Killam">Albert Clements Killam</a> (August 8, 1903 – February 6, 1905)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Idington" title="John Idington">John Idington</a> (February 10, 1905 – March 31, 1927)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/James_Maclennan" title="James Maclennan">James Maclennan</a> (October 5, 1905 – February 13, 1909)</li>
<li>Sir <a href="/wiki/Charles_Fitzpatrick" title="Charles Fitzpatrick">Charles Fitzpatrick</a> (as Chief Justice, June 4, 1906 – November 21, 1918)</li>
<li>Sir <a href="/wiki/Lyman_Poore_Duff" class="mw-redirect" title="Lyman Poore Duff">Lyman Poore Duff</a> (September 27, 1906 – January 2, 1944)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Francis_Alexander_Anglin" title="Francis Alexander Anglin">Francis Alexander Anglin</a> (February 23, 1909 – February 28, 1933)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Louis-Philippe_Brodeur" title="Louis-Philippe Brodeur">Louis-Philippe Brodeur</a> (August 11, 1911 – October 10, 1923)</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Wilfrid_Laurier&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>Wilfrid Laurier appears as the leader of the Canadian civilization in the <a href="/wiki/4X" title="4X">4X</a> video game <i><a href="/wiki/Sid_Meier%27s_Civilization_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Sid Meier&#39;s Civilization VI">Sid Meier's Civilization VI</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
